- **Not chosen:** a lock on the main database, which would stop helpers and backups. A PID file, which goes stale and needs a timeout or a liveness guess. Binding a port as the lock, which depends on configuration, so two ports would still share a directory. `flock` through `bun:ffi`, which adds an unsafe boundary for what SQLite already does. Bun has no file lock in `node:fs`.

## D70. Send history is kept and its bodies are dropped; retention shares one paced loop

Decided by agents on 2026-11-01, for owner review.

- **Decision:** `sends` rows are kept for as long as the install runs. Thirty days after a row was created, a finished row (`sent`, `failed`, `skipped`, `unknown`) loses its prepared message: `payload` becomes NULL and the new `tracked_links` column keeps the row's tracked link paths and nothing else. `queued` and `sending` rows are never touched. Finished webhook deliveries go 30 days after their last change, and expired sessions go at once.
- **Why rows stay:** the row answers an old email's unsubscribe link and pixel (`uuid`, `kind`, `subscriber_id`, `list_id`), a bounce match (`uuid`, `to_email`), the frequency cap (`subscriber_id`, `kind`, `status`, times), at-most-once (`idempotency_key`, `(enrollment_id, step_id)`) and analytics (counts, and the joins from views and clicks through `template_id` and `step_id`). Deleting any of them breaks one of these for mail still sitting in someone's inbox, or silently lowers a reported total, and no cutoff age makes that safe.
- **Why bodies go:** compaction replaces [26,855 bytes with 245 in the measured case](../perf/B2-retention.md), and a message can reach 8 MiB with attachments. Nothing reads it after a row finishes except the open-redirect check on tracked links and manual resend. It is also personal content kept for no purpose, so dropping it is the data minimisation case as well as the size one.
- **Links:** the redirect only goes to links rendered into that message. So a compacted row keeps those exact paths, and a link from another message is still refused. A resent copy carries its parent's UUID and links, and the parent's row answers for them, so the rule covers `failed` and `unknown` rows too. A subscriber's deletion clears `tracked_links` with `payload`, so a wiped message's links stop redirecting, as they did.
- **Cost:** `failed` and `unknown` rows can be resent for 30 days. After that `POST /api/sends/:id/resend` answers 409, as it already does for a row with no message, and a resend that already happened still returns its child. A failure nobody looked at for a month is not a message worth sending as it stood. `to_email` stays on a compacted row: a bounce needs it, and D25 clears it when the person is deleted.
- **Deliveries:** 30 days, measured from `updated_at`, because the row's own finish is what starts the period. A deletion clearing a finished row moves `updated_at`, so that row stays up to 30 more days with its personal data already gone. The period gives an operator a month to look at a failing receiver through the deliveries list, and retries end after 24 hours, so nothing needs a row after that. **Not chosen:** `created_at`, which never moves but is not when the row finished; the two differ by at most a day.
- **Sessions:** a row goes when its expiry has passed, by the same reading as the owner: `expires_at`, or seven days from creation for a row with none. Nothing is removed that `session()` would accept. Rows denied for other reasons, such as a future `created_at`, are not expired and stay.
- **One loop:** one loop runs the owners' purges in turn, one owner per pass, at most 100 rows (50 sends and 4 MiB of payload, or one larger row) with a 250 ms pause after a pass with rows, and hourly when none has any. Each module still deletes its own rows with its own SQL. Back-to-back passes left sends 103 s late, and three separately paused loops left them 21 s late and falling further behind ([measurements](../perf/B2-retention.md)). With one loop no send fell behind at the 50 messages/s target. The cost is a slow first drain, about 2.3 hours per million rows when three owners share it, which is a one-time cost and needs nobody watching.
- **Indexes:** migration 14 adds an index for each purge. Sends and deliveries get a partial index, holding only what a pass can still take, so a finished history leaves it. Sessions get an expression index on the expiry (`expires_at`, else seven days from `created_at` in whole seconds) over every row, because the expiry lives inside the row's JSON; the purge also checks a row with no stored expiry against its exact millisecond time, so none goes early. A pass reads its next rows at the same cost at any table size.
- **Not chosen:** deleting old `sends`, for the reasons above. Keeping every body, which grows the database by the whole message for every send, forever. Copying rows to a summary table so the rows can go, because the analytics joins, unsubscribe and bounce lookups would all need a second path to keep one number right. Hashes of the links instead of paths, which hide nothing since a link's UUID is already in the mail, and the path is at most a line in the row. A setting for each period or batch, because nothing yet says what an operator would set it to.
- **Event retention:** it ran on its own at 500 rows with no pause and had the same risk of holding back sends. [D74](#d74-synchronous-attachment-sends-share-a-memory-budget-event-retention-joins-the-paced-loop) moved it into this loop.

## D71. Admin pages have a Content-Security-Policy, and the editor stays in the page

Decided by agents on 2026-11-01, for owner review. Amends D66.

- **Decision:** every HTML response under `/admin` carries one policy, set in one middleware (`src/admin/policy.ts`), so an error, login or reset page cannot leave without it: `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'; img-src 'self' data: blob: <root URL and favicon origins>; font-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; frame-src 'self'; form-action 'self'; base-uri 'none'; object-src 'none'; frame-ancestors 'self'`. No nonces or hashes: every script is a static asset, and the server's translations and configuration became a JSON data block read by `boot.js`.
- **Alpine:** the admin loads `@alpinejs/csp` at the same 3.15.12, in place of `alpinejs`. The standard build compiles expressions with `new Function`, so it needs `unsafe-eval`, which would also hand that to any injected script. The CSP build interprets expressions with its own parser, which refuses template literals, `?.`, `??`, arrow functions, spread, statements, globals, assignment to DOM objects, and any expression on an `<iframe>` or `<script>`. About 180 of some 1,200 expressions were rewritten to fit, with no change of result, and the few that need more are methods. `test/admin/policy.test.ts` puts every expression of every screen through that parser. An operator's or a future screen's expression has the same limit, and `x-html` does not exist there. It is a build dependency like Alpine was, and adds nothing to the server.
- **`style-src 'unsafe-inline'`:** kept for two things. A body keeps its `style` attributes in the editor, which is what a newsletter is made of. And a preview written to an `srcdoc` inherits this policy on top of its own, so its `<style>` would otherwise be refused. No authored `<style>` reaches the page (D66), and without a remote `img-src` or `font-src` a rule cannot carry data out.
- **`frame-ancestors 'self'`:** the media picker frames an admin page. `form-action 'self'` holds because login is a local form: OIDC is not implemented. `script-src 'self'` covers `/admin/custom.js`, and that alone would not hold: a browser runs any same-origin `text/html` or `text/plain` response as a classic script, so an archive page or a preview holding JavaScript would pass it. Every response therefore has `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, set once in `Router.handle`. An upload with a JavaScript extension is served as `application/octet-stream`, because `nosniff` lets a JavaScript type run. The file still downloads as before.
- **Editor containment:** the surface stays in the page. Squire reads global `document` and `window` throughout and has 80 `instanceof` checks that fail across realms, so driving a frame from the page needs a fork of it. Running it inside an `allow-scripts` frame needs the toolbar, dialogs, table tools, find highlights, media picker and selection state rewritten over `postMessage`, and `allow-same-origin` with scripts is no sandbox. That would put the upstream editing result at risk for a surface that now has the allow-list and this policy behind it. The frame would add only a separate `style` scope.
- **Remote resources:** opening a body requests nothing outside. An `img src` or `background` the policy would refuse is kept in a `data-rte-*` attribute while mounted, put back on the way out, on the clipboard included, and never stored that way. The image dialog takes such an image's size from what is stored, as it has no picture to measure, and Squire's resize handles are not offered on it: they measure the picture and would store `width: 0px`. A notice offers to show them, which reloads the campaign with `?remote_images=1`, and only that screen on that request adds `http: https:` to `img-src`; unsaved changes are asked about first. The policy is the boundary, so a `url()` in a kept `style` needs no CSS parsing here: it is refused too, and reports a violation in the console. This is a deliberate difference: listmonk loads them on open, which tells a third party who opened a body and when, and the body may be written by any API credential.
- **Sanitizer:** the allow-list stays and DOMPurify is not added. The corpus in `test/e2e/hostile-html.js` runs in Chromium with and without the policy, through mount, serialize, remount, undo and redo. It found no way through. Reading a copy of a short list is simpler to reason about than DOMPurify's configuration, and its defaults keep SVG, which nothing here needs. If a corpus entry ever gets through, that is the time to revisit it.
- **Head content:** a document's `<head>` never reached the walker, so `<style>` and `<title>` left without a mention. The warning now names them. A refused URL keeps its dialog open with a message, where it used to close it silently.
- **Not chosen:** `img-src https:` on the campaign screen, which loads remote images and `url()` on open and leaves a way out for anything that gets on that page. A client-side gate with a wide policy, which would need a CSS parser to find every `url()` and `image-set()`. A nonce per response, which adds nothing to static assets.
- **Limits:** Alpine directives in injected markup still run under this policy, with no violation: the CSP build interprets them, so nothing here refuses them, and `<span x-init="setTheme('dark')">` would run in the page's scope. The allow-list (D66) is the only control for that, which is why no `x-*` or `@` attribute passes it. The strict policy applies when the admin is reached at any address, with the root URL's origin added, so a wrongly set root URL shows media as held. A custom admin script cannot use `eval`, load from a CDN or call another origin. Public pages are unchanged, other than `/admin/login`, `/admin/forgot` and `/admin/reset`, which share the public shell and have this policy.

## D72. The ALTCHA widget is in-house, and replay state stays in memory

Decided by agents on 2026-11-02, for owner review.

- **Decision:** ALTCHA and hCaptcha work on the public form as in listmonk (B4). The server side is `src/captcha/captcha.ts`, with Web Crypto only: SHA-256 for the key derivation and HMAC-SHA-256 for the signature, no runtime dependency. The browser side is an original 280-line script served at upstream's path, `/public/static/altcha.umd.js`, so the form and the snippets Lists → Forms generates, including ones pasted from listmonk, keep working. A snippet on another site loads that script as a module, which a browser fetches in CORS mode, so the script gets the trusted-origin CORS header of `/api/public/*`, as upstream sends it there; an origin not in `security.trusted_urls` gets none and its widget does not load. `test/e2e/specs/captcha.e2e.js` pastes the generated snippet on a second origin, solves and subscribes, and checks the untrusted case; `test/contract/public.ts` checks both headers against the pinned upstream.
- **Wire format:** altcha-lib-go v2.4.1 as listmonk 6fcd95f calls it. The challenge is `{"parameters":{"algorithm":"SHA-256","nonce","salt","cost":1,"keyLength":32,"keyPrefix","expiresAt"},"signature"}`: 16-byte hex nonce and salt, a counter drawn from 0 to the complexity, the key prefix the first 16 bytes of SHA-256(salt ‖ nonce ‖ uint32be(counter)), expiry five minutes ahead, and the signature HMAC-SHA-256 hex over the parameters as canonical JSON (sorted keys). The form field `altcha` is base64 of `{"challenge":{"parameters","signature"},"solution":{"counter","derivedKey","time"}}`. A solution verifies when it is unexpired to the millisecond, its signature matches, the key derived from its counter equals `derivedKey` in lowercase hex, and its signature was not used before. The contract suite solves a challenge, subscribes and is refused on replay against the pinned upstream and here.
- **Narrower input:** only listmonk's shape verifies: a `keySignature`, `memoryCost`, `parallelism` or `data`, which listmonk never issues and would sign, refuses without a signature check, and the payload is at most 4 KiB. Unknown keys are ignored, as Go's decoder ignores them.
- **Replay state:** used signatures are a map in memory to their challenge's expiry, swept at most once a second, and at most 50,000 at once; when full, a solution is refused until some expire, never let through. No table is needed: the HMAC key is drawn per process, as upstream, so after a restart no earlier challenge verifies, and one process serves a data directory (D69). Reaching it takes 166 solved challenges a second for five minutes.
- **Widget:** the upstream widget is 119 KB, built with Svelte, and adds hash-wasm and a floating UI, all for the SHA-256 case listmonk uses. The in-house element has the same contract for this form: `<altcha-widget challenge>`, a checkbox, a hidden `altcha` field, expiry that resets it. It solves in up to eight blob workers through `crypto.subtle.digest`, or on the page if workers are refused, so it needs no `unsafe-eval`; a page CSP would need `worker-src blob:` for the workers. Unlike upstream's default it also solves when the form is submitted unticked, and submits after. It is English, as upstream's build is; a `strings` attribute takes upstream's JSON overrides. The public pages have no CSP, and hCaptcha's script from js.hcaptcha.com needs none.
- **hCaptcha:** fails closed on a network error, a timeout (5 s, as upstream), a non-2xx answer, an answer over 64 KiB, a redirect or an unparsable answer; upstream lets an unparsable answer through. The verify URL is a dependency, so tests answer from a local server and never call hCaptcha.
- **Not chosen:** vendoring the altcha npm widget (MIT, AGPL-compatible): a large Svelte bundle for one checkbox, and an unreviewed dependency on a public page. Storing used signatures in a table: it would outlive the key that makes them meaningful.

## D73. Sonnet workers, Opus lead and an Opus high reviewer

Decided by the owner on 2026-11-02; amends D68.

- **Workers:** workers, fixers and helpers use Claude Sonnet 5.5 at medium effort (`claudeAgent`, `claude-sonnet-5-5`, effort `medium`). It is the project default. Haiku is no longer used for any role.
- **Lead:** the chief of staff stays on Claude Opus 5.5 at medium effort.
- **Reviewer:** the one council reviewer is Claude Opus 5.5 at `high` effort, down from `xhigh`.
- **Unchanged:** Anthropic models only and no Fable (D68), the reviewer's independence, council size and rounds, local verification, and everything else in D56 and D57.
- **In flight:** delegates already running keep their model; every dispatch after this decision uses the new targets.

## D74. Synchronous attachment sends share a memory budget; event retention joins the paced loop

Decided by agents on 2026-11-02, for owner review (B6). Amends D70.

- **Budget (amended by [D87](#d87-the-tx-attachment-budget-counts-measured-memory-b26): 128 MiB, counted in measured memory with new multipliers):** `POST /api/tx` keeps one byte budget per process, 64 MiB (`src/tx/gate.ts`). A multipart body (the only way to attach caller files) is charged five times its declared `Content-Length`, or five times the 5 MiB cap when none is declared, before it is read: the chunks, the joined body, the form and the file copies all exist at once. A synchronous send then holds its attachments, caller and template images together, once per recipient until SMTP answers, as the journal decodes a copy for each. A send without attachments is not charged. An `async` send is charged its encoded copies from the journal write until the request ends (D87), and holds nothing while SMTP runs later; it can still wait or be refused during the parse of a multipart body. A JSON request carries no caller files, is not charged for its body, and is charged only for template images, as before.
- **Over the limit:** an upload waits up to 2 seconds, in arrival order, for room; then it gets `429` with `Retry-After: 1`, before anything is journaled, so the caller's retry is not a second send. Growing a reservation after the parse (more recipients than the body cost) never waits and answers `429` at once, which is why no holder waits on another and the gate cannot deadlock. At most 16 requests wait; the 17th is refused at once, so waiting itself holds nothing unbounded. A request over the whole budget, which the 16 MiB request cap already refuses, is refused too.
- **Why 429 and not 503:** the condition is the caller's concurrent load on this endpoint and passes in seconds; 503 stays for the restore hold (D67). The earlier embedded-image check used 429 with the same header, and now shares the gate.
- **Event retention:** the standalone loop (500 rows, no pause) is gone. The events owner is a fourth purge in D70's loop, at 100 rows a pass and a 250 ms pause. Measured with 400,000 expired events and a send written every 20 ms in the same process, the standalone loop left sends a median 4.4-5.1 s late; the shared loop left them as on an idle process (p95 11 ms against 6 ms idle). The cost is the drain rate: about 380 rows a second where the standalone loop took 13,300, so a million-event backlog takes about 45 minutes and nobody needs to watch it. The steady-state rate at the 100 events/s target is 100 rows a second, a quarter of this loop's capacity before the other owners need it. [Measurements](../perf/B6-send-media-safety.md).
- **Send bodies expire 30 days from `created_at`.** D70 already said so; the line in `04-sending.md` that implied 30 days from finishing was the error and is corrected. Creation never moves, so a row's expiry cannot be extended by a late retry or a resend of its parent, and for every finished row the two dates differ by at most the retry window of a day. D70 is otherwise unchanged.
- **Not chosen:** a hard concurrency count alone, which ignores that one 4 MiB upload and one 1 KiB upload differ by thousands of times in memory. Streaming multipart parsing straight to the journal, which would remove the parse copies but changes the body reader every `POST /api/tx` shares. A separate event loop with a pause, which D70 measured as the worse of the two ways to pace retention. A setting for any limit, because nothing yet says what an operator would set.

## D75. Amazon SES bounces through SNS

Decided by agents on 2026-11-02, for owner review (B9).

- **Decision:** `POST /webhooks/service/ses` follows listmonk's route, setting (`bounce.ses_enabled`) and responses, with the behavior in [Sending](04-sending.md#bounces). No new dependency: `node:crypto` verifies RSA signatures and parses the certificate.
- **Beyond upstream:** upstream verifies SHA1 only (signature version 2 would fail), has no timeout or size cap on the certificate fetch, does not check the certificate's validity period or the subscribe URL, fetches the `UnsubscribeURL` of an `UnsubscribeConfirmation` (which ends the subscription), keeps only `destination[0]`, and stores the whole SNS message (headers, addresses, diagnostics) as `meta`. This version supports both signature versions, bounds the fetches, checks the validity period, accepts only an SNS host for the subscribe URL, acknowledges an unsubscribe without fetching, records up to the first 50 named recipients and keeps the codes plus a cut, address-free diagnostic, in line with D63.
- **Trust:** the certificate is trusted because it was fetched over HTTPS from an `sns.<region>.amazonaws.com` host of the pattern, as AWS's own guidance and upstream do; its chain is not validated against a CA store. A valid signature only shows that a message passed through SNS: any AWS account can confirm its own topic against a public endpoint and publish a bounce, and the first draft of this decision (like upstream) accepted it. Review found that one such message could blocklist or delete every subscriber it listed. The native setting `bounce.ses_topic_arns` (full topic ARNs, or `arn:aws:sns:*:<account>:*` for one account) is therefore required: after verification, a signed `TopicArn` not on the list is refused with 400 for confirmations and notifications alike, with no fetch and no write, and an empty list refuses everything. This is a deliberate difference from upstream ([API](07-api-and-ui.md#api-compatibility-policy)), and an operator must list a topic before SES works. A message records at most 50 recipients, SES's own limit, so a signed message from a listed topic cannot act on thousands.
- **Replays:** a message older than 24 hours is refused, and one dated up to 15 minutes ahead is accepted, so the receipt lasts until 24 hours after the later of the message's `Timestamp` and its arrival (D63): it outlasts every moment the message verifies.
- **Not chosen:** a certificate chain check against embedded roots, because the roots are Amazon's to rotate and a stale list would refuse real bounces. A custom fetch hook in the compiled executable, because an environment variable that redirects certificate fetching would be a way to forge bounces; the network is injected in tests only.

## D76. Empty `app.lang` and `upload.provider` are kept with a safe fallback; provider webhooks get their own budget; remote editor images need a same-origin navigation

Decided by agents on 2026-11-05, for owner review (B10).

- **Empty settings values:** a settings write that stores an empty `app.lang` or `upload.provider` (a partial XML or JSON write that omits them, as upstream's own tests and the XML projection do) is accepted, as upstream accepts it. Upstream then fails on its next reload (`app.lang` panic, "unknown provider" exit). Native does not: it starts with English and refuses uploads with 400. **Why not refuse the write:** the contract suite and real partial-write clients depend on the write succeeding, and nothing stored breaks a later start, so the "never accept stored data that breaks a later start" rule is met by the fallback. `test/settings/restart.ts` writes both empty, restarts the compiled executable and checks the page language and the upload refusal.
- **Provider webhook budget:** Mailgun and SES POSTs have one in-memory budget of 6,000 per client per minute, apart from the 300 of the unsubscribe forms. SNS delivers one HTTP POST per bounce; a 429 loses the bounce once SNS stops retrying. 6,000 is 20 times the old figure and still bounded: bodies are capped, and every request is signature-checked before anything is written.
- **Remote editor images:** `?remote_images=1` takes effect only for a navigation the admin page made itself (`Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin`). A link from another site, a mail or a pasted address gets the strict policy and the notice, so a crafted link cannot make the operator's browser fetch a campaign's remote images. A request that sends no `Sec-Fetch-Site` header is treated as before: browsers send it only to secure contexts (HTTPS or loopback), so on a plain-HTTP admin reached by a network address the button would otherwise do nothing. Only a header that is present and not `same-origin` refuses.
- **SES certificate host:** the region in `sns.<region>.amazonaws.com[.cn]` must look like an AWS region. Upstream's `[a-z0-9-]+` also accepts `sns.s3.amazonaws.com`, the S3 bucket named `sns`.

## D77. Other loops' commits do not explain the event tail; no change (B11)

- **Decision:** nothing changes in the event, outbox, scheduler or campaign commit paths. FULL durability (D22), at-most-once marketing sends and the no-network-in-transaction rule are untouched.
- **Evidence ([B11](../perf/B11-event-latency.md)):** a per-writer probe under the M6.1 mixed load shows the other loops hold the write lock about 11.5 s in 165 s, each commit at most 16.1 ms outside startup (campaign progress; the others at most 15.1 ms). Campaign progress, outcomes and claims already commit per batch. This does not support B7's guess that the other loops' commits cause the tail. The event writer's own transactions (29.3 s, including sequence evaluation) are not excluded.
- **`synchronous = NORMAL`:** one experiment sample, taken under host disturbance (load about 7, in-flight peak 16 against 8, max latency doubled), showed event p50 −37 to −41 %, tx accept p95 −14 to −19 %, tx delivery p50 −41 to −44 % and p95 −34 to −40 %, and event p95 21.9 ms, 1.1 ms below the FULL runs' 23.0 to 25.3 ms. Confirming this needs at least three alternating FULL/NORMAL runs on a quiet host. D22 is unchanged.
- **D22 decided in [D86](#d86-synchronous-stays-full-everywhere-b25).** Still open as engineering work, not an owner item: a process-model change. The CPU explanation for the tail is inferred by elimination and not measured; the process-model change has no measured expected gain.

## D78. Small tx sends overtake a waiting upload a bounded number of times; held-style merging keeps repeats and shorthand places

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-06, for owner review (B12).

- **Fair tx admission:** a send with attachments that holds nothing yet (JSON body, embedded images) used to take its few hundred bytes whenever they fit, ahead of a waiting multipart upload that did not fit yet ([D74](#d74-synchronous-attachment-sends-share-a-memory-budget-event-retention-joins-the-paced-loop)). A steady stream of them kept the room taken until the upload's 429. `ByteGate.admit` keeps that for the first 8 such sends per waiter at the head of the queue (`maxOvertakes`); later ones are refused at once with the 429 an over-budget send gets, before anything is journaled. They never queue: D74's rule that a waiter holds nothing (only uploads wait, before their body is read) stays true, and the 16 waiter slots stay the uploads'. **Why not queue them:** a queued send has already parsed its body and rendered every recipient's message, memory the budget does not count. **Why not strict FIFO:** a small send would be refused for every large upload in flight, which B10 chose against. Measured peak RSS in the fairness scenario is not higher than before (about 50 MiB lower at the end of the run): [B12-backlog](../perf/B12-backlog.md). The memory budget itself is unchanged.
- **Held styles in the editor:** `mergeStyle` no longer drops an editor declaration equal to an earlier one (`color:red;color:blue;color:red` stays as written); it removes only a held declaration present in both the style and its original (a paste). A kept shorthand the browser rewrote to its longhands (read from the browser's own parse) places them where the shorthand was. The splitter skips CSS comments (outside parentheses and quotes) and keeps them in the declaration they are in, and the property name ignores them.
- **Test infrastructure:** the build lock in `test/production-build.ts` is per checkout, records its owner's PID and clears only a lock whose owner is gone; a `mkdir` error other than `EEXIST` gives up after 5 s and a live holder after 15 minutes. `withProduction` accepts a readiness answer only when its own child process listens on the port (read from `/proc`, Linux only like the MVP), so a different server that took a reserved port cannot satisfy the retry.

## D79. A click on a held image puts the caret just after it (B13)

Decided by agents on 2026-11-06.

- **Decision:** a single click on a held image (`img[data-rte-src]`, D71) collapses the selection to the position right after it,. When the image is the last content of its enclosing link (a linked banner: nothing but whitespace after it inside the link) the position is right after the link instead, so typed text stays outside the link (B14). Typing then continues after the image, Backspace removes it as for any image, and ArrowLeft steps over it to the position before. In `<a><img> Download</a>` the caret sits inside the link after the image, so Backspace removes the icon and typed text joins the link text; in a banner, one Backspace removes the image with its emptied link: the click first drops only the whitespace-only text right after the image inside its link (never whitespace before it or between images), and only when the image is the link's last content (B15). For a banner alone in its paragraph, the editor drops Squire's end-of-block `<br>` once, the one after the text typed after the link, so it saves as `…</a>X</p>`; it stays armed after the click until it removes that `<br>`, Enter or another held-image click clears it, and it only removes a final, non-rendering `<br>` in a paragraph shaped link, text, `<br>`, so it never acts after Enter, in `<pre>` or on other content. Only ASCII space, tab, CR and LF count as collapsible whitespace (never `\s` or `trim()`, so U+00A0 and other Unicode spaces render and stay), and nothing is dropped when the link itself or an element around it has a computed `white-space` of `pre`, `pre-wrap`, `pre-line` or `break-spaces`. A link ending in U+00A0 or another Unicode space is therefore not a banner: a click changes nothing and the caret goes after the image inside the link, so typed text joins the link. The whitespace is dropped only when nothing visible follows the link in its block (text, an element, but not a final `<br>`): in `<a><img> </a>tail` the space separates the banner from `tail` and stays, so a click never changes that content (B16); whitespace that collapses with whitespace right after the link, as in `<a>\n<img>\n</a> tail`, is still dropped. A banner alone in a table cell keeps the trailing `<br>` Squire adds after typed text; it does not render, and the narrow paragraph rule is not extended to cells (B16). A click beside the image is the browser's own (tested, B14). A double-click keeps its selection on the image for the dialog.
- **Why:** a held image has no picture, so where the browser put the caret depended on the placeholder's box. The click already released a selected image (it must not reach Squire's resizer), which left no defined position.
- **Test:** `editor-containment.e2e.js`, "a click on a held image puts the caret after it, and an arrow steps over it (B13)"; B14 adds the linked image, Backspace and click-beside tests; B15 clicks right next to the image and adds the whitespace-banner Backspace and trailing-`<br>` tests; B16 adds the kept-space and table-cell `<br>` tests.

## D80. A `sends` lease is enforced while the server runs; a rejected push settles its rows (B18)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-06, for owner review.

- **Decision:** every outbox pass first moves `sending` rows whose `claimed_until` has passed to `unknown`/`interrupted`, with the same guarded transition and `ended` webhook as startup `recover` (one function, `interrupt`, in `sends.ts`). A row is never moved back to `queued`: sends stay at-most-once. Before, a stuck `sending` row held the subscriber's marketing cap until the next restart.
- **Lease length:** 120 s, for the loop and for `dispatch`. A push is cut off 60 s after it starts (`dispatch` in `email.ts`, an absolute deadline that also bounds connect, greeting and queueing), so a healthy push and its journal commit finish inside the lease. The old 60 s lease equalled that deadline.
- **Rejected push:** a push that rejects (or throws) journals its row `unknown`/`uncertain`, as the campaign runner does, in the loop path before it fails and in `dispatch` (which only logged it).
- **Guards:** `claimOne` updates only a `queued` row. A transient failure re-queues a row only while its payload exists; a row anonymized while `sending` (D25) becomes `skipped`/`subscriber_unavailable`, with no webhook, instead of being reclaimed and failing as `payload_unavailable`.
- **Not an API difference:** listmonk has no send journal, so there is no deliberate-difference row.
- **Tests and measurement:** `test/sends/lease.test.ts`; [B18](../perf/B18-outbox-lease.md).

B23 follow-ups (agent, 2026-11-07, for owner review):

- **Unsent push:** a push that throws before any pool sees the message never left. Under the restore hold (D67) its claim goes back to `queued` and the attempt is not counted; the loop claims nothing until release, so it cannot spin. Any other synchronous throw journals the row `failed`/`push_failed` and still fails the caller. A rejection after the pool accepted the message stays `unknown`/`uncertain`. Each `UPDATE` names `status='sending'` and the claim's `claimed_until`. `test/sends/unsent-push.test.ts`.
- **Clock jump over the lease:** `deliver` keeps the ids of the rows it is pushing, and the pass's expiry skips them (`expireLeases(time, live)`), so a forward jump of the injected clock cannot settle a live send under its own push. The push's own outcome (bounded at 60 s by `email.ts`) settles it. Crash recovery is unchanged: a row no push of this process holds, or any row at startup, still expires. A push that hangs past its cutoff would hold its row until restart; the emailer's deadline makes that a bug, not a state. [B23](../perf/B23-send-lease-followups.md).
- **Stale campaign runner counts:** a runner that loses its lease mid-batch writes nothing, so messages it did send are recorded `unknown` by the next holder's `abandonClaims` and are missing from `sent` (an undercount, never a double send). Counting them would need a write under a lost lease, which the lease exists to forbid.

## D81. A blocklisted subscriber holds only unsubscribed memberships (B19)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-06, for owner review.

- **Decision:** one store function (`blockOne` in `src/subscribers/store.ts`) is the way into blocklisted for the single and bulk routes, the automatic bounce action `blocklist`, PATCH and PUT with `status=blocklisted`, and the public unsubscribe's blocklist box. It stamps the subscriber's `updated_at`, ends every membership as `unsubscribed`, and stamps the membership's `updated_at` once for each row that really changed (the unsubscribe chart's date). The query-selected blocklist does the same set-based. The bounce action also queues `subscriber.unsubscribed` for the memberships it ends, as the bulk and PATCH blocklists do (`04-sending.md`). No path then gives a blocklisted subscriber `unconfirmed` or `confirmed`: PUT without a status, add by IDs, add by query and the opt-in page all leave the membership `unsubscribed`. Un-blocklisting leaves the memberships unsubscribed.
- **Existing databases:** migration 0016 sets every membership of a blocklisted subscriber to `unsubscribed`. It is a data repair: it does not stamp `updated_at` and queues no webhooks, since no consent changed when it ran.
- **Add by query keeps upstream's semantics (alignment with add by IDs dropped):** upstream's two queries disagree (`add-subscribers-to-lists` replaces an existing status when one is given; the `-by-query` variant is `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`). Making them agree would let one bulk add (the dashboard's select-all with preconfirm) re-subscribe every unsubscribed member of a list, so add by query stays `DO NOTHING` and add by IDs stays as upstream. Only the blocklist guard is added: a query add never gives a blocklisted subscriber a live membership.
- **Why:** upstream's automatic bounce action blocklisted the status alone, and PATCH without lists did too, so un-blocklisting resumed mail to people who had been blocked for bouncing or complaining; the other paths disagreed about the timestamps the chart reads. A consent rule that depends on which path ran is a bug.
- **Deliberate difference:** the bounce `blocklist` action (upstream's `record-bounce` block1) and PATCH `status=blocklisted` without `lists` now unsubscribe the memberships; add by IDs, add by query and PATCH with `lists` leave a blocklisted subscriber's memberships unsubscribed instead of confirmed or unconfirmed. Marked in the contract suite (`bounces.ts`, `subscriber-state-revisions.ts`, `subscribers.ts`).
- **Tests:** `test/subscribers/consent.test.ts`, `consent-query.test.ts`, `consent-migration.test.ts`, and the bounce and unsubscribe tests.

## D82. A double opt-in list cannot become single under unconfirmed members; the public form skips archived lists (B19)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-06, for owner review.

- **Opt-in:** `PUT /api/lists/:id` refuses a change from `double` to `single` with 400 and no write while the list has an `unconfirmed` member, and the admin form shows the message: it would start mailing people who never confirmed. Single to double is allowed, as upstream allows it: it only stops delivery to unconfirmed members and enables the opt-in confirmation campaign. The "delete unconfirmed" maintenance purge is an explicit admin action, so it is not a reason to refuse. Other fields, and the same `optin`, still save.
- **Archived lists:** the public form and `POST /api/public/subscription` ignore archived lists as the form already hides them; a request naming only archived lists is refused with the no-lists-selected message. Upstream's `get-list-types` does not look at the status.
- **Out of scope, kept as upstream:** a public signup on a single opt-in list re-subscribes an unsubscribed member; `disabled` subscribers and `archived` lists still receive campaigns.
- **Tests:** `test/subscribers/consent.test.ts`; contract cases `d82-*` in `test/contract/lists.ts` and `archived-*` in `test/contract/public.ts`, marked as deliberate differences.

## D83. One enrollment status per ending reason; exit events apply to every sequence status (B20)

`sequence_archived` and `blocklisted` end an enrollment as `exited` on every path; the scheduler used `canceled` for them while the archive and subscriber paths used `exited`. `canceled` stays for `manual` and `subscriber_deleted`. An `exit_on` event ends enrollments of draft and paused sequences too, so resuming cannot revive an enrollment whose exit event already arrived. Every ending clears `claimed_until` and `retry_at`. Native behavior, with no listmonk counterpart.

**Legacy rows (B22):** migration 17 sets `status='exited'` on enrollments stored as `canceled` with `exit_reason` `blocklisted` or `sequence_archived`, so the `status` filter and the exited count include them. `updated_at` and `finished_at` are untouched.

## D84. Bounce actions are validated; TOTP follows the account state; an operator can clear TOTP (B21)

`bounce.actions` rules take `count` 1 to 1000 and `action` of `none`, `unsubscribe`, `blocklist` or `delete`, the ranges the admin form offers. Upstream stores anything, so a `count` of 0 recorded no bounce rows and fired the action on every bounce, and an unknown action did nothing; both are now 400. `update-user` clears `twofa_type` and `twofa_key` when `password_login` goes off or `type` becomes `api`, so an old key cannot return. `iglo.email reset-totp --user <name>` turns TOTP off with the server stopped and ends the user's sessions. The audit's webhook-postpone item did not reproduce through the API (a claimed row is not due before its lease ends, and `recover` clears leases at start), so `delivery-postpone` is unchanged. A rule already stored is checked only when a request changes it, so data an older build accepted never blocks saving another setting; new or changed rules are refused. `reset-totp` says so when the user has no TOTP.

**Existing databases (B24):** migration 18 rewrites a stored bounce rule whose `count` is 0 and whose `action` is one of the four to `count` 1, and clears `twofa_type`/`twofa_key` of API users and users with password login off. A count of 0 acted like 1 on the action (`num >= count`) but recorded no bounce row; 1 also records the first bounce, which is what the admin form's minimum means. A migrated `{count: 0, action: "none"}` rule therefore now records the first bounce and fires the `bounce` webhook, where it used to drop bounces silently; that matches every other `none` rule. Rules without an action, such as the empty `*` default, are left alone. Tests: `test/users/b21.test.ts`, `test/users/legacy-b24.test.ts`, `test/bootstrap/instance-lock.test.ts`.

## D85. A send anonymized while in flight ends without `send.failed` (B22)

If a subscriber is deleted or wiped while one of their sends is `sending`, the row is anonymized (`to_email = ''`, D25). When that send then ends `failed` or `unknown`, no `send.failed` webhook is queued, whatever the outcome. The `ended` hook checks the row's current address after the update, since the row it holds was read at claim time. This is the same rule B18 applies to a skipped retry (D80).

- **Why:** D61 lets a wipe keep only the numeric ID and UUID. The event would otherwise carry the send's list ID, which tied to the kept UUID reveals what the person joined, and its subscriber would be `null` so a receiver could not use it anyway. The row's status stays visible in the send journal.
- **Not chosen:** a `send.failed` with `subscriber: null` and no list IDs, which tells a receiver nothing it can act on.
- **Also covers an admin delete without a wipe.** The row cannot tell a wipe from an ordinary delete: D25 anonymizes the send on every deletion, so the rule silences the in-flight send of a subscriber an admin deleted with `wipe: false` too. The producing app still holds the send UUID from `POST /api/tx`, and `subscriber.deleted` is delivered. Owner review: confirm dropping the event for admin deletes is acceptable.
- **Tests:** `test/webhooks/wiped-send.test.ts` (`POST /api/tx` to a receiver, wipe true and false, and a restart; absence shown by delivery order against a control `send.failed`); `test/sends/lease.test.ts` (`failed` and `unknown` through the `ended` hook, and lease expiry in the outbox pass with the injected clock).

## D86. `synchronous` stays FULL everywhere (B25)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-07, for owner review.

- **Decision:** every connection keeps WAL and `synchronous = FULL` (D19, D22). No writer moves to NORMAL and no second connection is added. No code changes.
- **Why:** a commit at NORMAL can roll back after a power loss or OS crash (SQLite documents it; it is durable across a process crash). The outbox claim and the campaign cursor would then show a row or batch as unsent after an external send: a second marketing email, which D12 forbids. The event and tx-accept commits are 2xx-acknowledged; a lost one is a lost acknowledged write, and a latency gain alone does not justify that. The remaining writers (outcomes, webhook deliveries, settings, subscribers) lose recorded facts. The only writers that could take NORMAL (lease renew, maintenance, summary refresh) hold the lock for fractions of a second per run, so there is nothing to gain.
- **Per-connection split considered:** events and tx accept at NORMAL on their own connection, claims at FULL. By WAL ordering a later FULL commit also syncs the earlier NORMAL frames, so the claim guarantee would hold and no duplicate could follow. It does not help the acknowledged writes: the last event or tx accept before a power cut has no later FULL commit to sync it, and those two writers are where the whole gain is. It also needs a second handle in `events` and `tx` for transactions that today share one connection with sequence and outbox work. Rejected.
- **Evidence:** [B25](../perf/B25-durability.md): six alternating runs (F N F N F N) with the same compiled build and command as B11, hashes recorded. **All six were disturbed (host load 3 to 7, the rule is below 2), so there are zero clean pairs**; no gain is quoted, and no re-run is queued because none could change this decision. It showed NORMAL ahead in every pair (event p95 23.1 to 28.0 ms against 52.5 to 70.8 ms for FULL, tx delivery p50 18 to 20 ms against 40 to 139 ms), but FULL's latency followed the host's disk load (which itself counts sync waits), so the size of that gap is not established. B11's quiet FULL runs had event p95 23.0 to 25.3 ms. The decision does not rest on these numbers.
- **When to revisit:** a clean series that shows a gain on a path whose lost commit is neither an acknowledged write nor a possible second send, or a client-visible way for an event/tx sender to learn that a 2xx was not yet durable (not designed).

## D87. The tx attachment budget counts measured memory (B26)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-07, for owner review. Amends D74; the "owner call" the backlog kept on it is made here.

- **Problem:** D74's 64 MiB counted attachment bytes, but peak RSS grew about eight times as much (B6), roughly 500 MiB at the limit, on a host whose minimum is 2 GiB.
- **Where the bytes went** (one 4 MiB attachment, one recipient, high-water mark from `/proc`, [B26](../perf/B26-tx-memory.md)): about 17 MiB in the multipart parse (chunks, joined body, form, file copy), about 40 in the journal write (base64, the JSON text, SQLite's copy of a 5.6 MB row), and about 100 more when the claimed row was decoded for SMTP. Nearly all of the last was one regular expression in `decodePayload`: `^(?:[A-Za-z0-9+/]{4})*...$` keeps a backtrack frame per group, 140 MiB for 5.6 MB of text.
- **Copies removed:** that regex is now a flat character class plus a length check (the byte-for-byte round trip that follows it still rejects non-canonical padding); `decodePayload` no longer re-encodes the message it just decoded only to validate it; `encodePayload` encodes from a view of the attachment bytes instead of copying them first. One send of 4 MiB went from 163 MiB to 63 to 82 MiB of growth. **Not removed:** the parse copies (Bun's `formData` needs the joined body, and streaming parsing would change the body reader every `POST /api/tx` shares), the per-recipient payload text (the journal stores one row each, and `sends.ts`/`outbox.ts` are not this task's modules), and the concurrent push of every synchronous recipient with its decoded copy.
- **Honest charge:** counted bytes are now real growth. A multipart body is charged 11 times its size before it is read (the parse peaks near 4.3, and the rest reserves the one-recipient hold so a request admitted to parse does not find the room gone afterwards). A request with attachments is then charged 4 times the attachment bytes plus 7 per synchronous recipient (5 for `async`, which is only encoded here and sent later, in turn). The budget is 128 MiB: the largest send the 16 MiB request cap allows is 4 x 4 + 7 x 16 = 128, so one such send still fits an idle server (tested).
- **Result:** one large send on an idle server is 63 to 155 MiB of growth (before: 153 to 208). A sustained stream of 4 MiB sends peaks at 250 to 390 MiB over 16 to 40 requests (before: 300 to 585): the target of about 256 MiB is met for serial and one-recipient bursts of smaller files and **missed** when two 4 MiB sends overlap, because the collector lets native memory pile up for seconds; `Bun.gc(false)` after each send changed nothing, so it was not kept. A 2 GiB host stays well inside this; a 1 GiB one would not.
- **Refusal trade-off:** two 4 MiB uploads at a time fit the budget (11 x 4.2 each) where D74's 64 MiB admitted three; a request for fifteen recipients with a 1 MiB file is charged 109 MiB, so a second upload while it is held waits or gets `429`. Waiting, `Retry-After`, no journaling before the answer and D78's overtaking rule are unchanged. Callers that send many recipients with attachments should use `async`.
- **Also new:** an `async` JSON send whose template embeds images is now charged too (main never refused it), so under load it can get `429`. A request whose charge exceeds the whole 128 MiB budget can never be admitted, even on an idle server, so it is refused before charging with the `400 invalid message` main gave it (a payload over 4 MiB), not a `429` a client would retry forever; only a charge that could fit later gets `429`. Example: two recipients and 7.5 MiB of template images (135 MiB). Tested through the HTTP API.
- **Not chosen:** a smaller budget, which would refuse the largest legal single send; calling the collector by hand; changing `sends.ts` to encode and insert one recipient at a time. Retention rate (380 rows/s) is unchanged.
- **Tests:** `test/tx/tx.test.ts` (a three-recipient hold refuses a second upload at 30 MiB, failing before the change; the largest sends fit idle), `test/tx/payload.test.ts` (decode memory, failing before the change on 147 MiB; canonical base64 only).
- **B28 amendment (2026-11-07):** with request bodies streamed by the harness (none held before the baseline), 40 x 4 MiB at once measured 265, 302 and 266 MiB, still over target. Three copies stayed reachable while SMTP answered and are now released: the request's parsed attachments (cleared before `dispatch`), the claimed row's journal text (`row.payload`, nulled after admission) and `Claimed.message` (dropped when the emailer takes it). The same burst is now 212, 224 and 234 MiB: **under 256 MiB**, so the budget and `MAX_ATTACHMENT_SENDS` stay. Formats, at-most-once and D78 unchanged. Evidence: [B26 doc, B28 section](../perf/B26-tx-memory.md).

## D88. The event tail is fsync waits plus `settings.read()` CPU; no change in events or sequences (B31)

Decided by an agent on 2026-11-07, for owner review. Completes D77's open question ("split event-loop delay by cause").

- **Decision:** `src/events` and `src/sequences` do not change; neither is the cause. The tail is split between two main-thread costs ([evidence](../perf/B31-event-tail.md), a loaded host, every run disturbed):
  - **COMMIT waits** (about 46 % of loop-blocked time; the events writer alone 29 %, 7,678 commits, mean 4.8 ms). `bun:sqlite` is synchronous, so an fsync blocks HTTP accepts too. D86 keeps FULL; moving writers to a worker or process is a process-model change that this measurement does not justify while the throughput target is met.
  - **CPU in `settings.read()`** (`bindFull` folds field names with a nested `find`, quadratic; 38 % of main-thread time in the window, `foldField` alone 30 %). It is called on hot paths (per request, per campaign push, per scheduler decision). An experiment that precomputes the fold map (not committed, outside this task's modules) gave event p95 44.7 and 43.5 ms against 58.8 to 70.0 for the controls, two runs each.
- **Corrects D77:** "other loops do not explain the tail" stands for their commits, but the CPU explanation was by elimination; it is now measured: settings re-binding, email/campaign work and the commit waits.
- **Follow-up:** B32 fixes `settings.read()` in `src/settings` (fold map, then caching until the settings table changes), with tests and a before/after run on a quiet host.
- **Probe:** `test/perf/m61/tail-probe.ts`, opt-in through `M61_TAIL_PROBE` / `M61_CPU_PROF` in `mixed.ts`; not in production paths.
- **Outcome (B32, [evidence](../perf/B32-settings-read.md)):** a lazily built folded-name map per static shape removed the quadratic lookups (event p95 58 → 41 ms, compiled, load 1.5 to 2.5). The profile after that still showed `settings.read()` at about 16 % inclusive, so `read()` now caches the projection while the stored rows are byte-identical. Every call still runs the one `SELECT key, value FROM settings`, so a write by any path (update, `writeStoredValue` for the restore hold and upload path, another connection, a restore) is seen at once; no counter or trigger, no migration. The cached object is deep-frozen, and `update` projects from fresh rows. p95 fell to 33 ms. The sending hold reads its row directly (`sendingHeld`), not through settings, so it never sees the cache.

## D89. Narrow, complete product scope (owner, 2026-11-07)

Directed by the owner on 2026-11-07. Amends D3, D42 and the scope table.

- **Goal:** feature complete but not wide. Everything a typical newsletter, lifecycle or transactional project needs, with one way to do each thing. Remove a second way to do the same job when it costs maintenance for no owner use.
- **Platforms:** Linux x86-64 and Linux arm64 only. macOS and every other target are dropped, not deferred (amends D42).
- **Docker:** an official multi-arch (amd64, arm64) image that wraps the release executable is needed now, not after 1.0 (amends D3). It adds no second build: the image copies the same compiled executable.
- **Templates:** no visual (drag-and-drop) editor. Editing templates and campaign content through MCP, so AI agents can do it, is in scope instead.
- **Sending provider:** Amazon SES only, for sending (over the existing SMTP pool) and for bounces and complaints (SNS). The Mailgun webhook is removed; Postmark, SendGrid, POP3 bounce scanning and postback messengers are dropped.
- **Deferred, not now:** translations other than English, OIDC login.
- **Dropped by agents under this goal:** S3 media storage (filesystem is the one way), ZIP import (CSV is the one way), the listmonk Postgres importer (no data to import, open question 3).
- **Decided by the B36 audit** ([12-completeness-audit.md](12-completeness-audit.md), #88): campaign archive/RSS and unique-count and list analytics are kept (present). Queued: B37 shared SES send-rate cap, B38 admin notification mails, B39 MCP completeness, B40 removal of inert settings controls, B41 deliverability docs. Dropped: A/B subject testing; thumbnails (`thumb` is the original image); an admin audit log; a Prometheus `/metrics` endpoint; runtime for inert compatibility settings (S3, OIDC, `maintenance.db`, non-SES bounce keys, `app.check_updates`, SMTP retry fields), which stay stored for D57 with no UI after B40; a DKIM admin UI (SES Easy DKIM is the one way); delivered/opened/clicked webhooks (SMTP acceptance is not delivery; opens and clicks are in the analytics API); dynamic segment targeting on campaigns (the query-to-list bulk action is the one way); runtime DNS checks of sending domains (SES verifies identities).
- **Implemented by B33:** `iglo.email-linux-x64` and `iglo.email-linux-arm64` are built and verified (`bun run check`, `release:verify`, the production contract) each on a native runner, `ubuntu-24.04` and `ubuntu-24.04-arm`, in `release.yml`; `ci.yml` (manual only) has the same two-runner `check` job. `release:package` names the file by the architecture it runs on, and the publish job merges the two directories into one `SHA256SUMS`. The `Dockerfile` copies the verified executable for `TARGETARCH` into a digest-pinned `gcr.io/distroless/cc-debian12` (glibc, libstdc++, no shell; its only `RUN` is a data-directory stage on the build host, so no emulation). On a `v*` tag the `image` job pushes `ghcr.io/iglo-tech/iglo.email:<version>` (and `:latest` without `-`) with `packages: write` on that job only; a manual run builds without pushing. The darwin entries of the reference harness are gone. Amends D42's "existing platform-selection code may remain".

## D90. Gemini 3.8 Flash does the work; Opus checks it (owner, 2026-11-07)

Directed by the owner on 2026-11-07. Amends D68 (Anthropic only) and D73 for workers.

- **Decision:** every worker, fixer, tester and helper runs on Gemini 3.8 Flash: `{providerInstanceId:"antigravity",model:"gemini-3.8-flash",options:{thinking_level:"high"}}`. Coding, testing, fixes, measurements and audits go to it.
- **Opus 5.5 only checks:** the lead (Opus medium) plans, briefs, reads results and makes sure the work was delivered as asked, with no gaps and no code-quality issues; it does not write code, including small review fixes, which go to one Gemini fixer per round. The single fresh council reviewer stays Opus 5.5 high.
- **Why:** save tokens; the owner wants the cheaper model to do the bulk of the work.
- **Switch:** delegates already running finish on their model; every new dispatch after this decision uses the new target. No Sonnet or other Claude model for implementation; no other provider. If Gemini 3.8 Flash is unavailable, record the block and wait for capacity.
- **Amended 2026-11-07 (owner):** Gemini 3.8 Flash always runs with `thinking_level:"high"`.
- **Brief quality:** because the worker model changes, briefs keep carrying the lead's file/line findings and explicit acceptance; the reviewer and lead check scope, tests and quality more strictly, not less.

## D93. Haiku 5.5 replaces Gemini as the worker model (owner, 2026-11-08)

Directed by the owner on 2026-11-08 ("resume the work using haiku 5.5 as worker instead of gemini"). Amends D90.

- **Decision:** every worker, fixer, tester and helper runs on Claude Haiku 5.5: `{providerInstanceId:"claudeAgent",model:"claude-haiku-5-5",options:{effort:"high"}}`. The lead (Opus 5.5 medium, plans and checks, writes no code) and the single fresh Opus 5.5 high reviewer are unchanged from D90.
- **Why:** the Antigravity (Gemini) quota ran out about every few hours on 2026-11-07/08, stalling B34, B35, B37 and B39.
- **Effort:** `high`, carried over from D90's high-reasoning rule; the owner named only the model.
- **Switch:** the quota-blocked Gemini B39 worker was cancelled and B39 restarted on Haiku from `origin/main`. The lead still re-runs format, lint, typecheck and the touched tests before review (Gemini twice reported checks it had not run).

## D94. Sequence steps send a transactional template; no step body on a campaign layout (lead, 2026-11-08)

Decided by agents (B47) for owner review, under D89.

- **Decision:** a sequence step keeps sending one complete transactional template, with an optional subject override (`05-sequences.md`, `src/sequences/input.ts:161`). Steps get no body of their own composed into a campaign layout. The design gap noted in #91 is closed as a drop, not a task.
- **Why:** D89 asks for one way to do each job. A step body on a campaign layout would be a second way to compose sequence content next to transactional templates, with its own editor, storage column, MCP tool fields, render path and tests. The one thing it buys, a layout shared across several emails, is also available today: transactional templates are complete messages, and since D89 AI agents edit templates through MCP (D91, B39 `update_template`), so changing a shared header or footer in each lifecycle template is one agent request. A typical lifecycle project has a handful of steps, so the duplication is small.
- **Cost accepted:** a lifecycle email does not inherit later changes to the newsletter's campaign layout automatically; its template must be edited too.
- **Revisit when:** the owner asks for shared layouts, or a project's sequences grow past a few dozen steps. Then the smaller change is template composition (one layout template referenced by transactional templates), not step bodies.

## D91. MCP endpoint for template and campaign editing (lead, 2026-11-07)

Lead design decisions from brief B34 ([D89](#d89-narrow-complete-product-scope-owner-2026-11-07)). Widened by [D98](#d98-mcp-covers-every-operation-except-a-short-exclusion-list-lead-2026-11-13).

- **Scope:** No visual editor; provide an MCP (Model Context Protocol) Streamable HTTP endpoint so AI agents can edit templates and campaign content.
- **Transport:** Stateless Streamable HTTP at `POST /api/mcp` returning `Content-Type: application/json`. No SSE stream or `Mcp-Session-Id` state; `GET /api/mcp` returns 405 Method Not Allowed as permitted by the MCP specification for servers without streams. Native JSON-RPC 2.0 implementation for `initialize`, `notifications/initialized`, `ping`, `tools/list`, and `tools/call` without runtime dependencies. Request body is size-bounded to 2 MiB like other API endpoints. Validates incoming `Origin` against the configured root URL origin and rejects foreign origins with 403.
- **Authentication:** Standard API-user authentication (`Authorization: token <user>:<key>` or Basic), integrated via `auth.middleware()`. Unauthenticated requests receive HTTP 401 before any JSON-RPC parsing.
- **In-process dispatch:** Every tool calls existing HTTP API endpoints in-process via `router.handle(app, request, peerAddress)` with the caller's authorization header and peer address. Tools may first read the stored object with the matching GET so a partial agent edit never clears fields (`update_campaign` performs read-modify-write, and `send_campaign_test`, `preview_campaign`, and `campaign_text` read stored fields when omitted). `update_sequence_step` does the same with the whole sequence, because `PUT /api/sequences/:id` deletes steps it omits: it sends every stored step back with only the given field changed. Caller-supplied values are passed to the endpoint unchanged, so the endpoint's validation and error messages apply; tools filter only values read back from storage. The sequence PUT has no version check and rewrites the whole definition (name, trigger, exit_on, reentry, list_id and every step), not only the steps. An edit made between the tool's read and its write, to any of these parts and from the UI, the API or another agent, can be overwritten. `status` is not sent, so a concurrent status change is kept. The agent should read the sequence again before a change it depends on. Tool output returns both `structuredContent` and text representation. Non-2xx responses from endpoints result in tool errors with `isError: true` and the API's error message. `src/mcp` owns no SQL tables or direct database operations.
- **20 Tools** (B39 added the last six, so an agent can do a whole editing job without the UI):
  - Templates: `list_templates` (`GET /api/templates`), `get_template` (`GET /api/templates/:id`), `create_template` (`POST /api/templates`), `update_template` (`PUT /api/templates/:id`), `delete_template` (`DELETE /api/templates/:id`), `preview_template` (`POST /api/templates/preview` for unsaved body, `GET /api/templates/:id/preview` for saved), `set_default_template` (`PUT /api/templates/:id/default`).
  - Campaigns: `list_campaigns` (`GET /api/campaigns`), `get_campaign` (`GET /api/campaigns/:id`), `create_campaign` (`POST /api/campaigns`, a draft; `lists` and `media` are the IDs from `list_lists` and `list_media`), `update_campaign` (`PUT /api/campaigns/:id`, `media` optional), `preview_campaign` (`POST /api/campaigns/:id/preview` or `GET /api/campaigns/:id/preview`), `campaign_text` (`POST /api/campaigns/:id/text`), `send_campaign_test` (`POST /api/campaigns/:id/test`).
  - Lists and media (finding IDs to target or attach): `list_lists` (`GET /api/lists`, `query`, `page`, `per_page`), `list_media` (`GET /api/media`, `query`, `page`, `per_page`).
  - Sequences (step content is message content, as campaign bodies are): `list_sequences` (`GET /api/sequences`), `get_sequence` (`GET /api/sequences/:id`), `update_sequence_step` (`PUT /api/sequences/:id`, changes one step's `subject` or `template_id`). Steps are identified by their ID from `get_sequence`. Editing an active sequence is allowed, as in the HTTP API ([sequences](05-sequences.md#sequence-definition)).
  - Reference: `template_reference` static reference generated at build time from `docs/design/06-templates.md`.
- **Polish (B44, 2026-11-08):**
  - *Read before write:* a read-merge tool needs the read permission as well as the write permission. Without it (`campaigns:get` or `campaigns:get_all` for `update_campaign`, `send_campaign_test`, and the partial-input cases of `preview_campaign` and `campaign_text`; `sequences:get` for `update_sequence_step`), the tool returns an error that names the tool and the read permission, and writes nothing. Skipping the read when the input looks complete was not taken: `update_campaign` and `update_sequence_step` need the stored object for most calls, so one rule is simpler and easier to explain.
  - *Clear send_at:* `update_campaign` advertises `send_at` as a string or null. `null` clears the schedule and is passed to the PUT; an omitted `send_at` keeps the stored one.
  - *No-op step edit:* `update_sequence_step` with neither `subject` nor `template_id` returns "Nothing to change" before any read or write. Saving would rewrite the definition and bump `updated_at` with nothing changed.

## D92. One shared send-rate cap for SES (lead, 2026-11-07)

Lead design decisions from brief B37 (B36 audit #88, [D89](#d89-narrow-complete-product-scope-owner-2026-11-07)).

- **Setting:** One new native setting, `messaging.max_send_rate`: messages per second for the whole process, default `14` (the AWS SES production default; sandbox is 1/s), `0` = no cap. Validated as an integer 0–10,000. Configured in Settings > Performance.
- **Why one cap:** Amazon SES enforces one account-wide maximum send rate across all connections. Previously, only campaigns were paced (`app.message_rate` per worker × `app.concurrency`), while transactional and sequence sends from the outbox were unpaced and uncounted together. When SES throttles (`454 Throttling failure: Maximum sending rate exceeded`), sequence steps (marketing at-most-once) are lost as failed, transactional messages incur retry backoffs, and campaigns risk hitting error limits. One shared send-rate limiter paces all outgoing traffic (campaigns, transactional, and sequence steps) before messages reach the SMTP pool.
- **Limiter injection:** One limiter object (`createSendRateLimiter` in `src/messaging/rate.ts`) is created in `src/main.ts` with the injected clock and dynamic rate getter `() => settings.read()["messaging.max_send_rate"]`, and injected into `createEmailer`. No module-level state (AGENTS.md).
- **Algorithm & pacing:** Smooth interval pacing (`interval = 1000 / rate`) so that within any sliding 1-second window no more than `rate` messages start. Token intervals smooth sends out evenly across the second, avoiding burst clustering at second boundaries. Setting changes apply immediately on the next wait without dropping queued waiters or losing accumulated tokens.
- **Priority:** Dual-priority queue where outbox sends (`priority: "outbox"`, transactional and sequence steps) take precedence over waiting campaign messages (`priority: "campaign"`). A large background campaign broadcast cannot delay an urgent transactional email (such as a password reset) by more than one token interval. Campaign workers retain their own concurrency and sliding-window pacing on top; the global cap only ever slows sends.
- **Clean shutdown:** Token waits use `clock.sleep` with an abort signal. Stopping the emailer or closing the app aborts pending waits cleanly; no message is sent after stop, and shutdown does not hang.
- **No new retries:** Marketing and sequence sends remain strictly at-most-once. The shared pacing cap prevents throttling rejections at the root rather than retrying dropped marketing mail.
- **Polish (B43, 2026-11-08):**
  - *Rate changes:* a rate change applies to the next grant. The next start is one interval of the new rate after the last start, both when lowering and when raising (`wake`), so a lower rate never leaves an old, shorter slot in place. No start history is kept, so a one-second window that straddles a lowering can still hold up to the old rate's starts (never more than the larger of the two rates).
  - *Settings listener:* `settings.onUpdate` takes one listener per store, and a second registration throws. A silent replacement would drop the limiter's wake; passing `wake` through the route layer would miss updates from other callers. `main.ts` registers once.
  - *SMTP settings test:* it is an interactive test of one message, but it is not exempt from the cap. It waits at most 10 s of injected time for a token, at outbox priority, then answers 503 "not sent" without sending, so an operator sees a clear reason and nothing is marked failed. A bypass would add an unpaced message to the account rate.
  - *Closed emailer:* a push after `close()` is not started (`not-started`), and so is a waiter refused by `close()`. The runner and the outbox claim nothing once the emailer is closed. A batch that meets a not-started outcome pushes no later recipient, and hands its unsent recipients back to the cursor. No delivery ends `failed` because the emailer was closed under it. Main's shutdown order (runner, outbox, emailer) still applies.
  - *Outcome mapping:* the outbox maps `not-started` to a held row (`queued`, attempt not counted). The `not-started` check in `sends.ts` `finish` is removed, so there is one path.

## D95. Uploaded PDFs stay inline; phone viewers are not checked (lead, 2026-11-09)

Decided by the lead after the owner asked who can upload media; for owner review.

- **Decision:** keep serving PDFs inline without the CSP sandbox (as listmonk does) and do not test iOS/Android PDF viewers.
- **Why:** uploading needs `media:manage` (`POST /api/media`), which only the super admin has unless the owner grants it to a limited role. A hostile PDF could at most let such a limited account act as a full admin when one opens it, and B60 showed that desktop Chromium and Firefox give PDF JavaScript no page, cookie or network access. For a single-owner instance that is not worth a device test or making PDF links download.
- **Revisit:** if a limited role that is not fully trusted gets `media:manage`, serve PDFs as `Content-Disposition: attachment` instead.

## D96. The release waits for the browser suite; no retries (lead, 2026-11-12)

Lead decisions on the three open points of the release gate in [13-e2e-coverage.md](13-e2e-coverage.md) section 5 (brief B71).

- **Decision:** `release.yml` has an `e2e` job that runs `E2E_WORKERS=2 bun run e2e` on `ubuntu-24.04`. `publish` needs it, and so does `image`, on tags and dry runs alike. Three rules follow:
  - **No retries.** `test/e2e/playwright.config.js` keeps `retries: 0`. A flaky test is fixed, never retried.
  - **A tag gate, not a merge gate.** The job runs on tag pushes and manual dry runs. CI stays manual-only and is not a merge gate for PRs (AGENTS.md, [D90](#d90-gemini-38-flash-does-the-work-opus-checks-it-owner-2026-11-07)); merges are checked locally by the worker and the reviewer.
  - **The restore-hold instance runs every time** (`operations.e2e.js`, B66), not only at release, because [D67](#d67-a-restored-database-holds-all-sending-until-an-operator-releases-it) is release-critical and the extra instance costs seconds.
- **Why:** a retry hides at-most-once and timing bugs, the exact failures this suite exists to catch. Releases are rare and must be proven, so a slower release is cheaper than a broken one. Merges are not gated because the suite takes about 11 minutes on two stacks and the worker and reviewer already check merges locally.
- **Considered:** one Playwright retry in the release job, which would hide flakes; running the suite on merge, which would slow every PR for a gate that is only needed at release.
- **Cost accepted:** a flaky spec blocks a release until its cause is fixed. The suite runs for the release tag on the x86-64 runner only, so an arm64-only failure is not seen here.
- **Revisit:** if a spec flakes on the runner, fix its cause or make it deterministic. Do not add retries.

## D97. The campaign runner wakes on start (lead, 2026-11-13)

A status change that makes a campaign runnable wakes the campaign runner at once instead of at its next 5 s scan: a start or resume (`running`), or a `scheduled` campaign whose `send_at` has already passed. The wake runs after the status write commits, and a failed wake is logged without changing the response. The result is the same as listmonk's (the HTTP response and what is sent do not change, and listmonk also waits for its scan, see [D57](#d57-compatible-client-results-and-safe-input-hardening)); only sending starts sooner. Pause and cancel are unchanged: they still take effect at the next batch or lease check.

## D98. MCP covers every operation except a short exclusion list (lead, 2026-11-13)

Owner, 2026-11-13: "I want the agents to be capable of doing everything for me there, maybe except stuff that is borderline dangerous. Any edits, configurations etc. should be exposed in the MCP." This widens [D91](#d91-mcp-endpoint-for-template-and-campaign-editing-lead-2026-11-07). Brief B78.

- **Decision:** `POST /api/mcp` exposes a tool for every operation in the API document (`src/openapi/operations.ts`) that no hand-written tool covers and the exclusion list does not take. The caller's role decides what runs, as for the HTTP API; the exclusions hold even for an admin credential.
- **Generated from the document** (`src/mcp/generated.ts`). The tool name is the operation id in snake_case, without a trailing `_by_id` when that name is unique: `updateSequenceById` is `update_sequence`, `getSubscriberById` is `get_subscriber`. `manageSubscriberListsById` keeps its suffix, because `manage_subscriber_lists` is the other operation's name. The description is the summary, the description and `Needs:` with the permissions. Path and query parameters are top-level arguments, and a JSON body is `body`. Every `$ref` is inlined, and a schema that refers back to itself becomes `{}`. Calls run in-process as the hand-written tools do (`router.handle` with the caller's authorization and peer address), so a refusal is `isError: true` with the API's message. **Every answer is capped at 1 MiB** (`MCP_MAX_ANSWER_BYTES`, `src/mcp/tools.ts`), JSON, CSV and HTML alike. The body is read as a stream before anything is parsed, and the stream is cancelled at the cap, so a larger answer is never held whole. The tool then returns `isError`. For a JSON answer it says to narrow the answer with `page` and `per_page` or to fetch it from the REST endpoint (for example `get_subscribers` with `per_page=all` over the cap). For a CSV or HTML answer it names the REST endpoint to fetch it from (for example `GET /api/subscribers/export`). The cap applies to the hand-written HTML previews (`preview_campaign`, `preview_template`) and to `campaign_text` as well as to the generated tools.

- **Which form is returned.** A non-JSON answer is returned once, as text. A JSON answer under the cap is returned in two forms: its parsed value as `structuredContent`, and the same value as compact text (`JSON.stringify`, no pretty-printing). Each copy is at most 1 MiB, so the reply is bounded at about 2 MiB. `structuredContent` is kept because D91 fixes it as part of the tool output, and the hand-written read-merge tools read it. The text copy is kept because the MCP spec says a tool that returns `structuredContent` SHOULD also return it as text.
- **Hand-written tools stay, and take every field of their operations** (B79). The 20 names are unchanged. `src/mcp/coverage.ts` maps the 20 operations they fully cover, so no generated tool repeats them. A hand-written tool that covers an operation takes each query parameter and body field of it, with the schema the generated tool builds (`src/mcp/tools.ts`), so `create_campaign` can make an opt-in campaign and `list_lists` filters by `type`. Each tool keeps its own argument names and schemas. Where an argument is an API field under another name, that name is the only one: `dir` is the `order` of `getCampaigns`. `update_sequence_step` changes one step, so the whole `PUT /api/sequences/{id}` is generated as `update_sequence`.
- **Path arguments** are checked against the operation's path schema before anything is sent (`src/mcp/generated.ts`). A numeric ID is a positive integer. An enumerated value is one of its values. A string is never `.` or `..`, written or percent-encoded, because the URL parser removes those segments and would send the request to another route (`delete_subscriber_bounces` with `id: ".."` was `DELETE /api/bounces`). The built path must also parse back to the operation's route with one non-empty segment per parameter. Any failure is `isError: true`, and nothing is sent.
- **Uploads.** `uploadMedia` and `importSubscribers` take `filename` and `content_base64` (the file, base64), plus the operation's other form fields (`params` is the JSON string the document defines). The MCP body stays at 2 MiB (`src/mcp/routes.ts`), so **a file is at most 1 MiB decoded**, and the tool refuses a larger one before anything is sent. Larger files go through the REST API (media accepts 25 MiB there). Raising the MCP body limit to 25 MiB was not taken: every `tools/call` would then buffer that much on the MCP route.
- **Excluded** (37 operations, one reason each in `src/mcp/coverage.ts`):
  - Not agent actions: the health and document endpoints, the MCP endpoint, anonymous public signup and captcha, and the provider bounce webhooks.
  - Privilege: create, change or delete users and roles; the caller's own profile and login; logout; two-factor setup, which is browser-session only. Reads of users and roles stay.
  - Bulk or irreversible: bulk deletes of subscribers, campaigns, lists and bounces; deletes, blocklists and list changes addressed by query; every `DELETE /api/maintenance/*`.
  - Whole-settings replace, `PUT /api/settings`. Each key changes through `PUT /api/settings/{key}`, and `GET /api/settings` stays.
- **Annotations** (MCP `annotations`, on every tool). `readOnlyHint` for GET and for `preview*`/`validate*` POSTs. `destructiveHint` for DELETE, blocklists, campaign status changes and `PUT /api/settings/{key}`. `openWorldHint` for what sends mail or calls an outside URL or starts it: campaign status, test sends, `POST /api/tx`, resends, enrolments, events, SMTP test, sequence activation and rescheduling, `subscriberSendOptin`, admin creates and updates of subscribers (they can send the opt-in mail), and webhook registration. A tool never retries, so a send is made once.
- **Not changed:** `src/openapi/*`, the 2 MiB body limit, and the sending rules. A tool does not add any way to send mail that the HTTP API lacks.
- **Test.** `test/mcp/generated.test.ts` covers the path refusals (IDs and external IDs, encoded and not, with nothing sent), the 1 MiB answer limit, and asserts that every operation in the API document is exactly one of a hand-written tool, a generated tool, or excluded; that the tool list is exactly those; that excluded names are unknown tools even to an admin; that schemas hold no `$ref`; the annotations; and a round trip through MCP with an admin credential (list, subscriber, sequence, enrolment, setting by key, webhook, media, CSV import). A restricted role gets the API's 403 as `isError`, and nothing is written.
- **Test (B79).** `test/mcp/handwritten-fields.test.ts` reads each covered operation's query parameters and body fields from the API document, and asserts through `tools/list` that each is an argument of its tool. It also round-trips `create_campaign` with `type: "optin"` and the archive fields, `list_lists` with `order_by`, `order` and `type`, `list_campaigns` with `dir`, and a test send rendered with `template_id`.
